  - `StandaloneSubAssemblies` (2026-09-15), scaffolded by `dotnet ef migrations add` and left as it came (the user's instruction: never hand-write a migration): `OwnerProductId` becomes nullable (EF drops and re-creates `IX_SubAssemblies_OwnerProductId_Name` around it, unfiltered). **Its scaffolded `Down` can't run once a standalone sub-assembly exists**: it sets the missing owners to 0, which no product has, so the FK rejects it. Verified up, down and up on a restored copy of `TinapayanRMS_Demo` (no standalone rows yet; the column came back nullable and the index unfiltered), then applied to the dev database after a `COPY_ONLY` backup.
  - `SubAssembliesAllStandalone` (2026-09-23), scaffolded by `dotnet ef migrations add` and left as it came: drops the owner FK, `IX_SubAssemblies_OwnerProductId_Name` and `OwnerProductId`, makes `Name` NOT NULL, and adds a unique `IX_SubAssemblies_Name`. It needs every owned row converted first (the one-off script under SubAssembly's Existing data): an unconverted one would come out nameless, and the unique index would refuse the migration, not lose it quietly.
  - `ProductOwnedRoles` (2026-09-23), scaffolded and left as it came: `SubAssemblyRoles.IsProductOwned` (bit, default 0, with `UpdateData` rows setting the seeded roles' to false), and `SubAssemblies.OwnerProductId` (nullable) with its index and Cascade FK to `Products`. No data step: what the flag does to existing sub-assemblies is `SubAssemblyRoleRepository.UpdateAsync`'s, done by ticking the roles in the app.
  - Always run `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes` after adding a migration, and rebuild Infrastructure before `database update --no-build` — a build from before `migrations add` doesn't contain the new migration, and EF then reports pending model changes.
- **Off-screen renders verify the card layouts without touching the user's monitor:** a file-based app (`#:project` the UI csproj) creates `new App()` + `InitializeComponent()` for the resources, builds the real ViewModel against a database (read-only), selects an item, and renders through a `VisualBrush` so the scrolled detail column comes out in full. Match `MainWindow`'s size mode: `Default` since 2026-09-15, which a new window already has (before that, `SfSkinManager.SetSizeMode(window, SizeMode.Touch)`, or inputs rendered at the compact height). For demo data, render against a restored copy, never the dev database.
- **After a command changes a row's server-computed fields (Phase 5: `DeletePriceCommand` changing `Ingredient.CurrentCost`/`CostPerGram`), refresh that one row rather than reloading the whole screen**: re-fetch it via the repository's `GetByIdAsync`, `_cache.AddOrUpdate(refreshed)` to update the grid, and reassign the `Selected*` property to the refreshed instance — `SfDataGrid` tracks selection by reference, so leaving `Selected*` pointing at the stale object silently desyncs the form/detail panel from what the grid now shows.
- **`UpdateAsync` must apply fields one by one onto a tracked, freshly-loaded entity — never `dbContext.Set<T>().Update(incoming)` on an object the caller built from scratch.** `Update()` marks *every* property Modified, so an `Ingredient`/`PackagingMaterial` built by a Save command's form fields (which never sets `CurrentCost`/`CostPerGram`, since those are server-owned) would silently overwrite them back to null/0 on every plain rename — a real bug found in Phase 6 while wiring `WeightLossPercent` through the same path, present in both `IngredientRepository` and `PackagingMaterialRepository` since Phase 4 and fixed in both at once. The fix is the same shape every time: load the tracked entity by id, copy across only the user-editable fields, leave every server-computed field untouched, then `SaveChangesAsync`. A related, easy-to-miss corollary: if a user-editable field feeds a server-computed one, the `UpdateAsync` that edits it must recompute that derived field too — it doesn't get another chance to until the next price entry. Originally `PurchaseUnitQty` feeding `CostPerGram`; since the multi-unit work it's an alternative unit's multiplier, which `RawMaterialUnitRepository.UpdateAsync` applies to the cost (and, for ingredients, to recipe-line grams).
- **`ExecuteDeleteAsync` doesn't wrap a failure in `DbUpdateException` the way `SaveChangesAsync` does** — it issues a raw `DELETE` outside the change tracker, so a foreign-key violation surfaces as the driver's own exception (`Microsoft.Data.SqlClient.SqlException` on SQL Server) instead. A test asserting `ThrowAsync<DbUpdateException>()` around an `ExecuteDeleteAsync` call needs `SqlException` instead (found in Phase 6, verifying `IngredientBomLine`'s Restrict-delete FK).
- **FluentValidation (Phase 13) is a thin, UI-facing pre-check layer added *alongside*, never in place of, the existing `ApplicationCore` Rules classes.** One `AbstractValidator<T>` per entity that actually has an editable Save form (`Ingredient`, `IngredientUnit`, `PackagingMaterial`, `PackagingMaterialUnit`, `SubAssemblyRole`, `SubAssembly`, `Product`, `CostingParameters`, `IngredientPriceHistory`, `PackagingPriceHistory` — ten total, the two unit validators added with the multi-unit work; read-only lookups like `IngredientCategory`/`PackagingCategory`/`UnitOfMeasure` deliberately got none, since there's no Save command to guard) mirrors the same rules the repository already enforces via `if/throw`. A Save/Add command runs the validator first and, on failure, sets `ErrorMessage` and returns *before* calling the repository — the repository's own checks are untouched and remain the authoritative last line of defense, so nothing about the already-tested defensive checks changes; FluentValidation only turns an invalid Save into an immediate, friendly on-screen message instead of a thrown exception the user never sees the text of. A "replace-all" set of lines or usages (the usages and packaging lines the Products Save sends, and the BOM lines the Raw Materials Save sends) gets no validator of its own — that cross-row validation already lives in the `ApplicationCore` Rules class the repository calls.
- **`ViewModelBase` (Phase 13)** — a shared `ReactiveObject` base class, introduced only once four CRUD ViewModels needed identical behavior (not before): `ErrorMessage`/`HasError` (an `ObservableAsPropertyHelper` derived from `ErrorMessage`, safe here specifically because `ErrorMessage` is itself a clean `RaiseAndSetIfChanged` property, not one driven by imperative collection mutations — see the OAPH lesson above), `IsBusy`, and a ReactiveUI `Interaction<string, bool> ConfirmDelete` the View fulfills with a real dialog. Two protected helpers wrap every command that touches the database: `RunSafelyAsync(Func<Task> action)` sets `IsBusy` for the call's duration and maps three exception types to a friendly `ErrorMessage` (`InvalidOperationException` → `ex.Message` verbatim, since every Rules class already writes a clear message; `DbUpdateException` → `"That name is already in use."`, the only scenario that type is ever thrown for anywhere in this app; `SqlException` → `"This item is currently in use elsewhere and can't be deleted."`, matching the `ExecuteDeleteAsync`-throws-`SqlException` behavior documented above) and lets any other exception type propagate uncaught, since that would be a real bug, not a user-facing condition to mask; `RunWithConfirmationAsync(message, action)` shows the confirmation dialog first and only calls `RunSafelyAsync` if the user agrees. Every command that calls a repository Delete method is wrapped in `RunWithConfirmationAsync`; every Add/Update-calling command is wrapped in `RunSafelyAsync` (with a validator pre-check, per the FluentValidation point above, when one exists for that entity); a Set-lines/Set-usages command is `RunSafelyAsync` only; a command that only mutates an in-memory edit list (`Add*Line`/`Remove*Line`/`Add*Usage`/`Remove*Usage`) or `NewCommand` is left unwrapped, since it's reversible pre-Save and carries no exception risk. `ProductsViewModel`'s tab-link commands (`LinkTabCommand`/`UnlinkTabCommand`) and `CloneCommand`/`CreateVariantCommand` are `RunSafelyAsync` only, not confirmation-gated — none of them destroy data (unlinking specifically *materializes* the tab's data rather than removing it, per the Phase 10 note above).
- **Serilog (Phase 13)** writes a daily rolling file to `logs/tinapayanrms-.log` under the app's base directory, 31-day retention, configured once via a static `Log.Logger` built in `App`'s static constructor (so even a licensing failure in that same constructor gets logged) and registered in DI as a `Serilog.ILogger` singleton. Each `ViewModelBase`-derived class calls `logger.ForContext(GetType())` once, in the shared base constructor, to get a per-class-named logger without needing a separate DI registration per ViewModel. `App.OnStartup` also hooks `DispatcherUnhandledException`/`AppDomain.UnhandledException` to log any exception that reaches those handlers.
- **Confirmation dialogs stay in the View, via a shared extension method, not baked into each View's constructor by hand.** `ConfirmDeleteHandlerRegistration.RegisterNativeMessageBoxHandler(this ViewModelBase)` wires a `ViewModelBase`'s `ConfirmDelete` interaction to a native `MessageBox.Show(..., MessageBoxButton.YesNo, MessageBoxImage.Warning)` in one place; every affected View's constructor calls `viewModel.RegisterNativeMessageBoxHandler();` once, right after setting `DataContext`.
- **A "replace-all" editor's referenced-entity validation must run *before* `ExecuteDeleteAsync`, never only implicitly via a dictionary lookup or a database FK constraint afterward** — a real, latent bug found and fixed in Phase 14's coverage audit. `PackagingLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` and `ProductSubAssemblyUsageRepository.SetUsagesForRoleAsync` (both Phase 8) already got this right: they build a lookup of the referenced entities that actually exist and loop over the incoming rows checking each one, throwing a friendly `InvalidOperationException` immediately, all *before* the old rows are deleted. `SubAssemblyLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` (Phase 7) and `IngredientBomLineRepository.SetLinesAsync` (Phase 6) did not: both deleted the existing lines first and only discovered a bad reference afterward — the former via a plain dictionary indexer throwing a raw, unfriendly `KeyNotFoundException`; the latter via the database's own `Restrict` FK constraint throwing `DbUpdateException` at `SaveChangesAsync`. In both cases the existing, valid rows were already gone by the time the rejection happened, with no transaction to roll them back. Neither path had a test covering "the new set references something that doesn't exist," which is exactly why it went unnoticed since Phase 6/7. Fixed by moving the existence-check dictionary/set build (and a `foreach` loop throwing `InvalidOperationException` per missing id) to before the `ExecuteDeleteAsync` call in both repositories, matching the established correct shape, with a regression test in each asserting the *existing* lines survive a rejected replacement.
- **A native `ProgressBar`, not a Syncfusion busy-indicator control, for Phase 13's busy-during-save indicator** — deliberate: the app references no Syncfusion busy-indicator package, and adding one for one small piece of UX polish wasn't worth it. A plain `ProgressBar IsIndeterminate="True" Height="4"` bound to `IsBusy` via `BooleanToVisibilityConverter`, placed in a status-bar row above each screen's content alongside the `ErrorMessage` `TextBlock`, is the sanctioned native-control fallback per the Conventions section below.
  - **Retrofitting a status-bar row onto an existing `Grid` needs `Grid.Row` added to every pre-existing child, not just the new row itself.** WPF defaults an unset `Grid.Row` to `0`; inserting a new `RowDefinition="Auto"` above the existing content and giving only the *new* status-bar element `Grid.Row="0"` leaves every already-existing child (which never had an explicit `Grid.Row` before, back when there was only one row) silently defaulting to `Grid.Row="0"` too, overlapping the status bar. Caught and fixed in `IngredientsView.xaml` by re-reading the file after the first edit; applied correctly from the start in `PackagingMaterialsView.xaml` and `SubAssembliesView.xaml` afterward. `ProductsView.xaml` doesn't use a column-based `Grid` at all (its panels are a horizontally-scrolling `ScrollViewer` of `StackPanel`s, per the CRUD screen pattern note above) — for that shape, wrap the existing `ScrollViewer` in an outer two-row `Grid` (status bar in row 0, the untouched `ScrollViewer` in row 1) instead of touching every inner panel.
  - **Sending synthetic `WM_LBUTTONDOWN`/`WM_LBUTTONUP` directly to a window's `HWND` (via `SendMessage`/`PostMessage`, with screen-to-client-translated coordinates) doesn't reliably drive a Syncfusion `ButtonAdv`'s click, even though it targets the exact window and never touches the real screen cursor** (tried in Phase 13, verifying the new validation/confirmation wiring while the user had a game occupying the foreground — see the "can't win against a real foreground app" lesson above). Syncfusion's WPF controls also don't expose the standard UI Automation `InvokePattern` (`GetSupportedPatterns` on a `ButtonAdv` returns only `SynchronizedInputPattern`), so `AutomationElement`-based invocation doesn't work either. Both were dead ends for exercising a write-path command without either real OS focus or a real physical click — which matches, and reinforces, the existing Phase 5/7 guidance to rely on integration tests plus a line-by-line binding review for write paths when the app can't safely be given real focus.

## Reactive Costing Cascade

The core design problem is that a price change on any ingredient or packaging material must ripple up through everything that uses it, automatically. The cascade has two starting points, one per raw material type, and both end at the product:

```
Price entry added for an ingredient
  → purchased ingredient's CostPerBaseUnit (per gram) recomputed
    → every made ingredient that uses it recomputed, up every level of the BOM tree
      → every SubAssemblyLine using a changed ingredient recomputed
        → SubAssembly.TotalCost recomputed
          → every ProductSubAssemblyUsage of that sub-assembly recomputed ─┐
                                                                           ├→ Product total cost recomputed
Price entry added for a packaging material                                 │    → Product margin / selling price recomputed
  → packaging material's CostPerBaseUnit recomputed                        │
    → every PackagingLine using it recomputed ─────────────────────────────┘
```

**Quantity Required hangs off the end of that cascade** (2026-09-20), and adds nothing to it. It is a plain multiplier applied where the branches meet — `CalculateMaterialCost` multiplies the usages' cost per piece by it and adds the packaging once — so a product that sells in boxes of 8 moves whenever its piece does, with no propagator of its own. Nothing about it is stored derived, and no write path has to know it exists.

This replaced packs (2026-09-18 to 2026-09-20), which hung off the same end: a pack was priced from the product's material cost per piece by `PackMaterialCost = materialCostPerPiece × PiecesPerPack + that pack's own line costs`. The one cost that was a pack's alone was its own packaging lines (`PackagingLine.PackId` set), which rode the packaging branch exactly as the per-piece lines did, and `PackagingLineRepository.GetEffectiveForProductAsync` returned `PackId is null` lines only to keep the per-piece figure honest. With packs gone every packaging line is the product's, and that filter went with them.

The original idea was to model this with DynamicData `SourceCache<T>`s joined via `.Connect()`/`.InnerJoin()`/`.TransformMany()`, so derived observable collections update themselves. **That's not what got built.** Phase 6 established a different, simpler pattern first — a pure ordering function plus a stateful database orchestrator, described below — and it worked well enough that Phase 11 extended the *same* shape to the sub-assembly branch instead of introducing DynamicData joins partway through. EF Core stays the single source of truth throughout; nothing here lives only in an in-memory observable.

**Two costs go up the tree side by side** (since the buffered cost, 2026-09-14): the true `CostPerBaseUnit` and the `BufferedCostPerBaseUnit`. A purchased material's buffered cost is a fixed amount since 2026-09-19 (see Buffered cost under RawMaterial): `RawMaterialRules.ApplyBrandPrices` sets it only on a first price, and the Raw Materials Save writes it otherwise. Every write through the cascade sets both: `IngredientCostPropagator`, which runs `IngredientBomRules.CalculateCost` twice per made ingredient (true costs, then buffered costs — with nothing of its own on top since 2026-09-21, and its stored `CostBufferPercent` normalised to 0 in the same pass). Everything downstream reads the buffered one: `SubAssembly.TotalCost` (`SubAssemblyCostPropagator`, `SubAssemblyLineRepository`), sub-assembly line costs, and packaging line costs in Products and Reports. The one exception is on read only: the Products screen's **Unbuffered** switch (2026-09-29) prices what it shows and prints from the true `CostPerBaseUnit` instead. Nothing stored changes, and the propagators don't know it exists. A buffer edit is one more way a cost changes: `RawMaterialRepository.UpdateAsync` reprices a purchased material's buffered cost itself and asks for the cascade for any ingredient whose buffer changed (packaging needs none, as below).

The one nesting exception is made ingredients: their BOMs nest to any depth, so that part of the cascade can't be a fixed one-hop join. An ingredient's cost can change from a new price entry, a BOM line edit, a `WeightLossPercent` edit, or a linked ingredient's link or share edit; when it does, the made ingredients that depend on it need recomputing in dependency order, components before the ingredients built from them. Sub-assemblies don't nest (a `SubAssemblyLine` only ever references an `Ingredient`, never another `SubAssembly` — Phase 7), so that branch needed no ordering logic at all, just "recompute every sub-assembly touched."

**Ingredient branch — implemented Phase 6** as `IngredientCostGraph.GetDependentsInOrder` (`ApplicationCore`, pure graph logic, no I/O — reverse-BFS from the changed ingredient to find every made ingredient that transitively depends on it, then a DFS-based topological sort restricted to that set) and `IngredientCostPropagator.RecomputeAndPropagateAsync` (`Infrastructure`, the orchestrator: loads every ingredient and every BOM line once, calls the graph function, walks the resulting order recomputing each ingredient's cost per gram (`CostPerBaseUnit`) from its own lines — reading each component's cost directly off the already-loaded, already-correct dictionary, never re-walking that component's own tree — then a single `SaveChangesAsync`). Every write path that can change an ingredient's cost calls it: `RawMaterialPriceHistoryRepository`'s add/update/delete (for an ingredient's price), `RawMaterialRepository.UpdateAsync` when `Source == Made` (a `WeightLossPercent` edit), `RawMaterialUnitRepository.UpdateAsync` when an ingredient unit's new multiplier reprices it (its latest price is in that unit), and `IngredientBomLineRepository.SetLinesAsync`. (Before the raw materials merge these were `IngredientPriceHistoryRepository` and `IngredientRepository`.) It loads ingredients only (`Type == Ingredient`), since packaging never takes part.

**Linked ingredients needed no new ordering** (2026-09-16). A linked ingredient's link is one more edge in the same graph: `IngredientCostPropagator` adds `linked → linked-to` beside every `made → component` before calling `IngredientCostGraph.GetDependentsInOrder`, which is unchanged, so egg white is recomputed after the egg and a made ingredient using egg white after egg white. In the walk, a linked ingredient is costed with `RawMaterialRules.LinkedCost` from its linked-to ingredient's already-fresh true cost, takes that ingredient's buffer percent, and its buffered cost is its cost with that percent on top, and a made one with the BOM rollup as before. A linked ingredient that's itself the changed one is recomputed first, as a made one is: `RawMaterialRepository` asks for the cascade when a linked ingredient is added or updated.

**Sub-assembly branch — implemented Phase 11** as `SubAssemblyCostPropagator.RecomputeAndPropagateAsync` (`Infrastructure`, taking the *set* of ingredient ids whose cost just changed — no per-ingredient ordering needed, since sub-assemblies don't nest: finds every `SubAssemblyLine` referencing any of them, groups by `SubAssemblyId`, and recomputes each affected sub-assembly's `TotalWeight`/`TotalCost` from *all* of its own lines' current ingredient costs — not just the changed line — in one loaded-once-saved-once pass). `IngredientCostPropagator` calls it at the end of its own run, passing `changedIngredientId` itself (whose own cost the caller already updated, for a Purchased ingredient) unioned with every made ingredient it just recomputed — so this one call, from the existing three ingredient-branch write paths, now reaches every sub-assembly transitively affected, at any BOM depth.

**Product and packaging branches needed no propagation code at all.** Neither `ProductSubAssemblyUsage`'s cost-per-piece, `PackagingLine`'s cost-per-piece, nor a product's total material cost is stored anywhere (Phase 8's "derived values are computed on read" decision) — they're plain `ProductRules` functions reading `SubAssembly.TotalCost` and the packaging material's `CostPerBaseUnit` fresh every time a screen asks. As long as those two columns are correct — which the ingredient and sub-assembly propagators now guarantee, and which the price history repository has guaranteed for packaging since Phase 5 (`RawMaterialPriceHistoryRepository` since the raw materials merge, which skips the ingredient cascade for a packaging price) — a product's displayed cost is automatically correct too, with nothing to propagate into. Verified end-to-end (`ReactiveCostCascadeTests.cs`): a price change several BOM levels deep reaches a sub-assembly's `TotalCost` with no direct edit to that sub-assembly, and a product's live-computed cost-per-piece reflects it immediately, with zero product-level or packaging-level code involved. Since 2026-09-14 a usage's weight per piece is itself computed on read (`ProductRules.ResolvePieceSize`, from the typed figure and the current `TotalWeight`), so a product that typed a yield sees its piece weight, and so its cost per piece, move when the owner changes the batch — with nothing to propagate either. Sub-assembly ownership didn't change the cascade at all: it still runs per sub-assembly, and a linked product reads the same row.

**Composite products needed no propagation either** (2026-09-29). A composite stores only its lines (a product and a quantity). What each line brings is its product's recipe cost, and its packaging cost when the composite includes its products' packaging, worked out on read through `SavedProductReader` like the product's own figures, so a price change reaches a composite the moment it reaches the products it holds, however deep. The Products screen reads the lines' costs again with every `LoadCommand`.

**Add-ins needed no propagation either, for the same reason** (2026-09-16). A `UsageAddInLine` stores only `UsageId`, `IngredientId` and `Grams`; its cost is `Grams × the ingredient's BufferedCostPerBaseUnit`, read fresh, and no derived total about it is stored anywhere. So the two propagators are untouched, and `SubAssemblyCostPropagator` deliberately still recomputes a sub-assembly from `SubAssemblyLines` alone — an add-in must never move the batch of a sub-assembly another product owns. `ReactiveCostCascadeTests` pins both halves: repricing an add-in's ingredient moves the linking usage's cost per piece, and leaves `SubAssembly.TotalCost` exactly where it was.

This does mean a product **already on screen** only reflects a price change made from a *different* screen once the user reselects it (or navigates back to it) — there's no cross-screen live push. That's the one piece of the original DynamicData-joins vision this phase deliberately didn't build; revisit only if that specific UX gap is ever reported as a real problem, not preemptively.

## Development Environment

- Development happens directly on a local Windows machine that has the .NET 10 SDK and SQL Server Express (instance `.\SQLEXPRESS`, service `MSSQL$SQLEXPRESS`, Windows authentication) installed.
  - **Why SQL Server Express, not LocalDB** (2026-09-28, at the user's request). The app used LocalDB (`(localdb)\mssqllocaldb`) until then. The dev database was moved with a `COPY_ONLY` backup restored onto `.\SQLEXPRESS`, which kept its `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS` collation (the instance's own default is `Latin1_General_CI_AS`, so the test database, created by the fixture's `MigrateAsync`, gets that one; both are case-insensitive and accent-sensitive). Every table was checked identical afterwards. `AUTO_CLOSE` was turned off on `TinapayanRMS` and `TinapayanRMS_Test`: Express turns it on for its databases, and on a service instance it shuts an idle database down and empties its cache, so the next read after a pause is slow. It replaced an unrelated `TinapayanRMS` prototype database from before this repo (created 2026-08-31, with a `Recipe`/`RecipeVersion` schema), which the user chose to drop. LocalDB is still installed and holds its copy, unused.
  - A day earlier (2026-09-27) the app was briefly moved to PostgreSQL. That was reverted before it was committed (2026-09-28, at the user's request) and is kept only as a git stash.
- Running the app (not just building it) requires the `SYNCFUSION_LICENSE_KEY` user environment variable to be set (see Syncfusion UI Framework below). The solution builds fine without it, but `App`'s static constructor throws on startup if it's missing.
- **Docker is not used anywhere in the project**: not for development, testing, or deployment.
- **Debug builds write WPF's binding errors to the log** (`BindingErrorLog`, added in `App`'s static constructor, 2026-09-25). WPF traces them only while a debugger is attached, and the app is mostly run without one. They land in `src/TinapayanRMS.UI/bin/Debug/<tfm>/logs/tinapayanrms-<date>.log` as `WPF binding Error …` warnings.
  - **The first walk through every screen (2026-09-25, off-screen, against the dev database) found one real kind, now fixed.** Five `DoubleTextBox`es bound to a `decimal?` (Raw Materials' Weight Loss %, Weight Share %, Cost Share %, Cost Buffer % and Buffered Cost) threw in WPF's default decimal-to-double conversion each time the value was null: an `InvalidCastException` caught per update. Their bindings now set `TargetNullValue={x:Null}`, which hands a null straight to the box without converting. Any new `DoubleTextBox` over a `decimal?` needs the same.
  - **Expected at every startup, and harmless**: one `Cannot find governing FrameworkElement` or path error per `GridMultiColumnDropDownList.ItemsSource` or `GridCheckBoxColumn.AllowEditing` binding while its grid is built (a column isn't in the element tree until Syncfusion gives it the grid's `DataContext`; every column ended up with the right list or flag), and `TabItemExt`'s own `FindAncestor` warning from Syncfusion's template.
- **Off-screen harnesses** are .NET 10 file-based apps (`dotnet run Probe.cs`, referencing the UI project with `#:project`) that build `App` and `CompositionRoot` and show windows off-screen without activating them. The 2026-09-19 measurements and the 2026-09-25 leak and binding checks used them.
  - **`new App()` posts WPF's startup to the dispatcher**, so the first `Dispatcher.Run()` also runs `App.OnStartup`, which builds its own host and shows the real main window on screen (found 2026-09-25). Collect what `new App()` posts with `Dispatcher.CurrentDispatcher.Hooks.OperationPosted` and `Abort()` it before running the dispatcher.
  - Stand in for the main window with an off-screen `Window` themed like it (`SfSkinManager.SetTheme`). Otherwise the theme's shared brushes keep a strong reference to the first window that uses them, which reads as a leak.
  - `dotnet run` of a file-based app doesn't notice changes in a referenced project: pass `--no-cache` after editing the app.
  - Don't `ShowDialog` in a harness: it activates the window and takes focus from whatever the user is doing.
- EF Core migrations are generated with `dotnet ef migrations add`, not written by hand, and applied to the local database with `dotnet ef database update`. Run both from the repo root with `--project src/TinapayanRMS.Infrastructure`; that project's design-time `TinapayanRMSDbContextFactory` supplies the DbContext.
- **A phase is done** when the solution builds without errors, all tests pass, and any new migration applies cleanly to a local database. Mark it Done in the Build Plan table; there are no separate per-phase documents.
- **Integration test database**: tests never touch the developer's own `TinapayanRMS` database. Each test project that needs a database has one of its own. `TinapayanRMS.Infrastructure.Tests` uses `TinapayanRMS_Test_Infrastructure` and `TinapayanRMS.UI.Tests` uses `TinapayanRMS_Test_UI`. The name is set in the project's `Fixtures/DatabaseFixture.cs`, a one-line subclass of `TinapayanRMS.Tests.Common.Fixtures.DatabaseFixtureBase`, which overrides the connection string's `Initial Catalog` (same server). They can't share one because `dotnet test --solution` runs test projects in parallel, so one project's Respawn reset would wipe rows from under the other's running test. That was the single `TinapayanRMS_Test` database until the 2026-09-28 split. Each database is migrated once per test run, has `AUTO_CLOSE` turned off by the fixture (Express turns it on for a database it creates; see the SQL Server Express notes), and is reset with Respawn before every test via `[Collection(DatabaseCollection.Name)]` + `IAsyncLifetime`. Only EF's own `__EFMigrationsHistory` table is excluded from the Respawn reset (`DatabaseFixtureBase.TablesExcludedFromReset`). `UnitsOfMeasure` used to be excluded as well while migrations seeded it, and that exclusion went when the seed rows did (see the Units of Measure notes under Application Architecture). Add a table to that list if a future phase adds seeded lookup data that tests depend on.
  - **Every Respawn-backed integration test class inherits `TinapayanRMS.Tests.Common.Fixtures.PersistenceTestBase(fixture)`** instead of retyping the `CreateDbContext`/`CreateDbContextFactory`/nested `TestDbContextFactory`/`IAsyncLifetime` plumbing per file (that scaffolding was identical across 8 test classes before being extracted). `[Collection(DatabaseCollection.Name)]` still has to be repeated on every concrete test class, though — it's deliberately **not** on the base, since xUnit's collection/fixture discovery isn't guaranteed to honor an attribute declared only on a base class, and getting that wrong would silently break the very Respawn-reset isolation this hierarchy exists to guarantee. `DatabaseCollection` (and `DatabaseFixture`, which it binds) is defined in each test project's own `Fixtures/` rather than in `Tests.Common`, because xUnit only finds a `[CollectionDefinition]` in the tests' own assembly. Because each project's fixture keeps the name `DatabaseFixture`, a test class reads the same in either project.
- **Code coverage (Phase 14): `coverlet.collector` never actually worked in this repo and was removed.** It only integrates through the classic VSTest collector pipeline, but `global.json`'s `test.runner: Microsoft.Testing.Platform` setting (needed for `dotnet test` at all under the .NET 10 SDK — see Tech Stack above) runs tests through native MTP instead, where `dotnet test --coverage`/`--collect:"XPlat Code Coverage"` silently fails ("Zero tests ran") with `coverlet.collector` present. Replaced with `Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage`, the MTP-native coverage extension — `dotnet test --solution TinapayanRMS.slnx --coverage --coverage-output-format cobertura --coverage-output coverage.cobertura.xml` now works and writes a real Cobertura report to `TestResults/` (gitignored). Measured line coverage: `ApplicationCore` 99.4%, `Domain` 100%, `Infrastructure` excluding the auto-generated `Migrations` folder 97.2% (the raw tool number for `Infrastructure` reads ~32% because EF Core's generated migration `Up`/`Down`/`BuildTargetModel` methods — over 4,000 of the folder's ~5,300 lines — never execute under Respawn's reset-an-already-migrated-database model and aren't meaningfully unit-testable; exclude that folder by eye when reading the raw report, don't chase it). `UI` reads ~5% and is expected to, per the standing no-ViewModel-tests convention below.

### Open items (for later phases)


- **Adopting a Syncfusion control the app doesn't use yet.** The theme package already styles every Syncfusion control, so a new control only needs its `PackageReference` in `TinapayanRMS.UI`, at the same version as the other Syncfusion packages. (Through Phase 15 this took a three-step procedure against the trimmed `TinapayanRMS.Theme` project — obsolete since the theme became a package.)
  - If the control is built around Syncfusion's newer "Sf*"/`Syncfusion.UI.Xaml.*` theming framework (as opposed to the older `Syncfusion.Windows.Tools.Controls`/`Syncfusion.Windows.Shared` control set), **check the locally installed Syncfusion Sample Browser source before assuming a property works the way its name suggests.** Essential Studio installs its full demo source to `C:\Users\Public\Documents\Syncfusion\WPF\<version>\SampleBrowser\` (grouped by area, e.g. `navigation\NavigationDrawer\Views\*.xaml`), and it is the authoritative answer to "which properties is this control actually meant to be set with, together" — a question neither the property list, the XML docs, nor the generated theme XAML answers. Demo filenames don't always contain the control's name (`DisplayMode.xaml`, `Hierarchical.xaml`), so grep contents rather than globbing filenames. These controls can have several control templates selected by a mode property, each honoring a genuinely different subset of properties, so the "does this control support X" question is really "does the template for the mode I'm in support X".
  - **`SfNavigationDrawer` is the worked example** (Phase 15, `MainWindow`'s navigation drawer): it has two control templates selected by `DisplayMode`. The `Compact`/`Expanded` template draws the drawer's **own built-in hamburger toggle** (a `ToggleButton` two-way bound to `IsOpen`, so no toggle button of your own is needed) and renders the `Items` collection through a `NavigationView`. The `Default` template has **neither** — no toggle at all, and it presents `DrawerContentView` instead, ignoring `Items` entirely. Setting `Items` under `DisplayMode="Default"` compiles fine and the drawer's own chrome still themes correctly, so it looks like a theming problem, but nothing from `Items` ever renders. **`MainWindow` moved to `DisplayMode="Default"` on 2026-09-22** (at the user's request: the menu should be hidden until summoned, with no permanent rail), so the Default template's two gaps are now the thing to work around rather than avoid — see the sub-bullets below.
    - **The menu marks nothing as current** (2026-09-22, at the user's request: "highlighting an item in the Navigation Drawer as the currently selected is redundant"). The tab strip right below the drawer already shows the page showing, so the amber row was saying it twice. Removing it took the selection out of the design altogether: **navigation is driven by the row's click, not by the `ListBox`'s `SelectedItem`**, because an invisible selection would be free to drift from the tab actually open, and a click on a row that happened to still be selected raises no `SelectionChanged` — it would have closed the menu without navigating. That also retired `SelectMenuItemFor` and its `_syncingMenu` re-entrancy guard, which existed only to keep the highlight in step with the tab strip. Regression test worth keeping by hand: pick the same destination twice in a row; the second pick must still navigate and close.
    - **The arrows have to walk the rows by hand; `MoveFocus` does not work here.** A `ListBox` sets `KeyboardNavigation.TabNavigation` to `Once`, so `FocusNavigationDirection.Next`/`Previous` leave the list altogether instead of visiting the sibling rows — Down jumped straight past the destinations and the menu could not be driven from the keyboard at all (reported by the user, 2026-09-22, after the first cut shipped with `MoveFocus`). `MoveMenuFocus` indexes `NavigationMenu.Items` and calls `Focus()` on the next row instead. The arrows are handled in `PreviewKeyDown` so the `ListBox` never sees them and can't move a selection this menu doesn't use.
    - **Opening the menu focuses the `ListBox`, not a row**, so nothing lights up on open; the first Down steps onto the top row (`MoveMenuFocus` treats "nothing focused" as start-at-the-end-you-came-from). Focus then shows as the *same* `#14000000` wash as hover — an outline of its own was read as the "current page" marker all over again, which is what had just been removed.
    - **It stays a `ListBox` even with no selection**, rather than becoming stacked `ButtonAdv`s like the Add Sub-Assembly drop-down, and the reason is hover: a flattened `ButtonAdv`'s themed hover is an opaque `#FFF9F9F9` that its template sets on its own border, which beats any `Background` set on the control and is a 3-level grey step against this panel (measured in a screen capture, 252,252,252 → 249,249,249). `DrawerItemStyle`'s rows own their hover outright (`#14000000`, the wash `ToastCloseButtonStyle` already uses), so it actually reads.
    - **The toggle and the menu are hand-rolled.** The toggle is a flat icon-only `ButtonAdv` in `MainWindow`'s header row — *outside* the drawer, which lives in row 1, so the scrim never covers it and a second click closes the menu. The menu is a `ListBox` in `DrawerContentView` whose `ListBoxItem`s are templated by the window's own `DrawerItemStyle`, with Settings a `ButtonAdv` below it. A `ListBox` rather than the stacked `ButtonAdv`s the Add Sub-Assembly drop-down uses, because this menu needs a persistent "you are here" state, which is exactly a `Selector`'s `SelectedItem`; templating a *framework* control is fine, since App.xaml's no-`Style` rule is about Syncfusion controls, whose templates must stay intact.
    - **Each row needs `AutomationProperties.Name`.** Its content is a glyph and a label in a `StackPanel`, not a string, so UI Automation reads the row out as `System.Windows.Controls.ListBoxItem` without it (seen in the running app before it was added). `NavigationItem.Header` used to supply this for free.
    - **The size property swaps with `Position`, and both apply only in `Default` mode.** `DrawerWidth` sizes a `Left`/`Right` drawer, `DrawerHeight` a `Top`/`Bottom` one; under `Compact` neither is read (the drawer expands to `ExpandedModeWidth`), which is why the `DrawerWidth="220"` this screen carried from Phase 15 until 2026-09-22 never did anything. Defaults worth knowing: `CompactModeWidth` 40, `ExpandedModeWidth` 250, `DrawerWidth` 400, `DrawerHeight` 636, `Transition` `SlideOnTop`, `AnimationDuration` 100 ms, `Position` `Left`.
    - **`Position="Top"` was tried and reverted** (2026-09-22, same day: asked for on "the menu drawer slides from top to bottom", then reverted on "I don't like that the items inside the navigation drawer is now horizontally positioned"). The lesson worth keeping: a top drawer is **not** a narrow panel entering from above — `UpdateDrawerAnimation`'s `Position.Top` branch zeroes the horizontal offset and translates the panel down by its own height, so the drawer always spans the window width. That forces the destinations into a row, which is what the user rejected. If a top drawer is ever wanted again, the menu has to be laid out horizontally (a horizontal `ItemsPanel`, an underline marker instead of the left bar, Settings at the right end, a bottom edge instead of a right one) — it is not a one-property change.
    - **`MenuAdv` was considered as a replacement for the whole drawer and not taken** (2026-09-22, the user asked "why not just use MenuAdv"). Recorded so it needn't be researched again: `MenuAdv`/`MenuItemAdv` are `Syncfusion.Windows.Shared` in **`Syncfusion.Shared.WPF`** (not `Syncfusion.Tools.WPF`), already present transitively and covered by the existing `http://schemas.syncfusion.com/wpf` xmlns, so no new `PackageReference`. Windows11Light themes them fully (`MenuAdv`, `MenuItemAdv`, `MenuItemSeparator`, plus `SyncfusionMenuCheckBoxStyle`/`SyncfusionMenuRadioButtonStyle`), so unlike a plain `ListBox` they'd need no hand-templating. `MenuItemAdv.Icon` is `typeof(object)`, so a glyph `TextBlock` drops in as `NavigationItem.Icon` did, and `IsCheckable` + `CheckIconType="RadioButton"` + `GroupName` would give the current-page mark. **The catch: the theme's item template puts `IconContent`, `CheckBoxPanel` and `RadioButtonPanel` in one overlapping `IconGrid`, so an item shows a glyph *or* a check mark, never both** — and `OnIsCheckablePropertyChanged` drives those visibilities, so it isn't something markup can dodge. Note also that every shipped Sample Browser demo drives `MenuAdv` through `ItemsSource` + `HierarchicalDataTemplate` + `ItemContainerStyle`; none declares `MenuItemAdv` elements inline.
    - **`HeaderHeight` and `FooterHeight` are registered but never bound by the Windows11Light template**, and the three drawer views are just the `Auto`/`*`/`Auto` rows of one grid, so they size to their content. Only `DrawerContentView` is used here, with one `Border` giving the panel its padding and its one edge. `DrawerHeight` is left unset too: `RefreshSlider` derives it from `ActualHeight` and re-runs on `SizeChanged` (verified by maximizing and resizing with the menu open — the panel tracked the window every time).
    - **Hosting `NavigationView`/`NavigationItem` by hand doesn't work**, so don't reach for it to get `IsSelected` and `ItemType="Button"` back. Both types are public, but every one of those behaviours hangs off the internal `NavigationItem.navigationDrawer` back-reference that only `SfNavigationDrawer.UpdateItems` sets from the `Items` collection: `Border_MouseUp` raises `ItemClicked` only `if (navigationDrawer != null)`, it flips `IsSelected` unconditionally so items toggle instead of behaving like radio buttons, and `NavigationView.UpdateNavigationItemFocus` dereferences `sfNavigationDrawer.FooterItems` before its own null check, so Tab throws `NullReferenceException`. The types also carry `<exclude/>` and `[EditorBrowsable(Never)]`.
    - **There is no dimmed backdrop and can't be one** without retemplating: the scrim grid is hardcoded `Background="Transparent"` and the only knob, `SfNavigationDrawer.Opacity`, is `internal`. The drawer reads as floating by its own `CardBorderBrush` right edge instead.
    - **Clicking away closes the menu through that scrim**, whose `PreviewMouseLeftButtonDown` sets `IsOpen = false` — but only inside `if (flag)`. `flag` is set by `ToggleDrawer()` **and** by `OnIsOpenChange`'s open path, so `IsOpen = true` would work too; the app calls `ToggleDrawer()` anyway because it's the documented way in, it's what Syncfusion's demos use, and it clears the secondary drawer's state. Closing uses `IsOpen = false` directly, since those are unconditional closes and toggling would *open* a shut drawer.
  - **A `NavigationItem`'s icon goes in `Icon` (a real element), not `IconTemplate`, and the element has to center itself** — `HorizontalAlignment="Center" VerticalAlignment="Center"`, as every icon in Syncfusion's own `DisplayMode.xaml` demo does. The item template drops the icon into a fixed-size `ContentPresenter` with a negative left margin, so an unaligned glyph renders visibly off-center. (Still true of `Compact`/`Expanded`, but **no longer applies to `MainWindow`** since 2026-09-22: its glyphs sit in the app's own menu rows, aligned by `DrawerIconStyle`.) In compact mode the icon is the *only* thing visible for each destination, so every item needs one.
  - **`TabControlExt.CloseMode="Hide"` (Phase 16), not the default `Delete`, is what actually makes "close a tab, reopen it from the drawer" work.** The first implementation (Phase 15) left `CloseMode` at its default and had the drawer's click handler re-add the closed `TabItemExt` instance to `MainTabControl.Items` if it was missing (`if (!Items.Contains(tab)) Items.Add(tab);`). That compiled, selected the right content, and even looked right in a quick check — but a real close-then-reopen cycle (caught only once the user actually did it) showed the tab's *content* switching in correctly while its *header* never reappeared in the strip at all: `TabControlExt`'s header strip doesn't regenerate a header for a `TabItemExt` instance that has ever actually left `Items` under `Delete` mode, even when the exact same instance is re-added later. `CloseMode="Hide"` (`Syncfusion.Windows.Tools.Controls.CloseMode`, confirmed against `Syncfusion.Tools.WPF.xml`'s member docs) sidesteps the whole problem: a closed tab is only `Visibility.Collapsed`, never removed from `Items`, so `ShowTab` just needs `tab.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;` before selecting it — the instance was never invalid to begin with. This also fixed a smaller side effect of the `Delete`-mode workaround: a reopened tab used to land at the *end* of the tab strip (`Items.Add` appends) instead of back in its original position, since `Hide` mode never reorders `Items` at all. (Syncfusion also ships a fully built alternative for exactly this scenario — `TabListContextMenuOptions` including `ShowHiddenItems` on the tab-list dropdown menu, which lists and restores hidden tabs on its own — but the app keeps the `SfNavigationDrawer` as the single reopen affordance rather than offering two different ones for the same action.)

## Conventions

- **The user manual follows the app** (2026-09-27). `docs/user-manual.md` is written for the shop's staff: current behaviour only, labels in bold exactly as the screen shows them, amounts without a currency symbol, and no code names. A change users can see (a screen, button, field, menu item, rule or cost formula) updates it in the same change. PLAN.md keeps the history; the manual never mentions what used to be.
- The layered architecture is fixed: `Domain` has no external dependencies, business and costing rules live in `ApplicationCore`, and EF Core and other I/O stay in `Infrastructure`.
- The MVVM toolkit is **ReactiveUI**, not CommunityToolkit.Mvvm. Use `ReactiveObject`, `ReactiveCommand`, `WhenAnyValue`, etc.
- **Every file goes in the folder for its kind, and the folder is its namespace** (see Solution Structure). A new View goes in `Views/` with its ViewModel in `ViewModels/`, a new repository in `Repositories/` with its interface in `ApplicationCore/Interfaces/`, a new rule in `Rules/` with its test in `tests/…/Rules/`, and so on. Nothing goes at a project root except app-startup plumbing.
- **Prefer Syncfusion controls over native WPF controls whenever a Syncfusion equivalent is available** (e.g. `ButtonAdv` over `Button`, `TabControlExt` over `TabControl`, `ComboBoxAdv`/`SfDataGrid`/the `SfInput.WPF` edit controls over their native counterparts), so the whole UI picks up the Windows11Light theme consistently. Fall back to a native control only when no Syncfusion equivalent exists. Adopting a new Syncfusion control only needs its package added to `TinapayanRMS.UI` — the packaged theme already covers every control Theme Studio knows about.
- **Grid quantities show 1 decimal and take up to 4** (2026-09-18, at the user's request): every Qty, Qty per Purchase Unit and Grams column is `NumberDecimalDigits="1" TypedDecimalDigits="4"`, 4 being what the database keeps. On its own, NumberDecimalDigits caps both what a grid's DoubleTextBox editor shows and what can be typed into it, so 1 would round every quantity typed to a tenth. `NumericColumn.TypedDecimalDigits` needs `controls:NumericEditing.IsEnabled="True"` on the grid, whose `NumericEditRenderer` gives that column's editor MaximumNumberDecimalDigits (which only caps what's typed) in place of NumberDecimalDigits; the cell outside editing still shows the column's decimals, so 0.1667 reads 0.2.
- **No currency symbol on a monetary value** (2026-09-22, at the user's request): amounts are shown as bare numbers — `StringFormat='{}{0:N4}'` in XAML, `$"{cost:N4}"` in a ViewModel, `CurrencySymbol=""` on a `CurrencyTextBox` — and a field label names the money without a "(₱)" suffix. Decimals: **2 for every per-piece costing figure on the Products screen** — the header’s Material Cost and Base Cost, and the Costing card’s Sub-assemblies, Packaging, Labor, Overhead, Administrative, Subtotal, Markup and VAT, in the tile captions as well as the summary table (2026-09-22, at the user’s request), and the Packaging section’s header figure with them — 2 for a per-purchase-unit amount or a price, and 4 only for a per-base-unit cost (a raw material’s cost per gram, a packaging line’s unit cost) and for the recipe tabs’ own Cost per piece.
- **An input's label goes on its left in a stacked form** (2026-09-24, at the user's request: "Since this is a desktop app and not a mobile app, let's make a practice that the label/textblock be placed on the left of the TextBox for input"). Wherever fields stack one under another, each field is a row with its label in an `Auto` column (`FormLabelStyle` in App.xaml: muted, centred on its box, 6 px before it, narrowed from 12 at the user's request the same day) and the input in a `*` column. The label column lines up down the form with `SharedSizeGroup` under `Grid.IsSharedSizeScope` when the rows are separate containers. Done in Settings (Categories, Product Categories, Roles, Units, Costing Defaults), the Products Costing tab, Clone, the Recipe Structure form (source picker, Role and Name, the piece-size column), Reports and the New Category dialog, whose label is now just "Name". **Two exceptions, the user's choice:** side-by-side strips of fields keep `FieldLabelStyle` above the input, because a label beside every box doesn't fit one row at 1366 px. That now covers only the Linked Cost, Purchase Cost and Usage Example rows. The three screens' header fields were exceptions too until later the same day, when the user asked for their labels on the left as well. Each is one row, with each label in an `Auto` column before its field, 16 px under the header's read-only figures. **Products:** Product Name, Category, Quantity Required and Current Price. At 1366 px that leaves Product Name about 190 px, so a long name scrolls in its box. **Raw Materials:** Name, Type, Category, Source (an ingredient) or Base Unit (packaging, moved out of the Base Unit tile 2026-09-26) and, for a made ingredient, Weight Loss %, whose box went from 120 to 80 px wide to make room. **Sub-Assemblies:** Name and Role, which used to share a row with the batch totals. The totals (Batch Weight, Batch Cost, Cost per g, or a packaging set's Set Cost, first in its row) now sit above the fields, each label over its figure. Read-only figures keep a small label over the value: header figures and stat tiles.
- **Decimal precision**: keep `decimal` values at full precision through the whole cascade, and round only for display and for the final selling price. Rounding each per-piece cost before summing adds up to drift. Enforced once for every `decimal` column via `TinapayanRMSDbContext.ConfigureConventions` (`decimal(18,4)`, set in Phase 4 when the first `decimal` columns were added), rather than per-property — a future column can't silently fall back to SQL Server's `decimal(18,2)` default and truncate a fractional cost. **The exception is a material's costs per base unit** (`CostPerBaseUnit`, `BufferedCostPerBaseUnit`), `decimal(28,20)` since 2026-09-19 at the user's request ("it should be displayed in a rounded version to two decimal places. internally we will not limit the precision"): a buffered cost typed per bag is divided down to per gram, and 4 decimals would round it (₱1,297.37 a 25 kg bag came back ₱1,297.50).
- **Use modern C# 14 / .NET 10 idioms** rather than older patterns still valid in the language: primary constructors, collection expressions (`[..]`), required members, file-scoped namespaces, pattern matching, and `record`/`record struct` for immutable data shapes, in line with what's already in the codebase (e.g. `CategoryPersistenceTests`'s primary constructor, `WellKnownUnitOfMeasureIds`'s collection expression). Don't reach for an older, more verbose equivalent just because it's more familiar.
- **Reads through Dapper, writes through EF Core** (2026-09-18/19, at the user's request, following the "pragmatic CQRS" split in boldsign.com/blogs/ef-core-vs-dapper). **Every public repository read method is plain SQL run by Dapper**, returning the same entity objects (with the same navigations filled in) that the EF `Include` chains used to, so ViewModels, `ProductRules` and `CostingRules` didn't change and the repository interfaces didn't either. **Everything else stays in EF Core**: every write, the schema and every migration, and a read inside a save that either loads an entity to change it (it must be tracked for `SaveChangesAsync`) or checks a condition through an EF expression (`SubAssemblyRepository.GetLinkedByAsync(dbContext, Expression, …)` behind `EnsureNoneLinkedElsewhereAsync`, the existence and uniqueness checks). An untracked graph read that a save also needs is shared instead of duplicated: it takes the save's DbContext and runs through Dapper *in that context's transaction* (`DapperReads.Command` passes `Database.CurrentTransaction`), e.g. `PackagingLineRepository.ResolveEffectiveAsync`, used by both `GetEffectiveForProductAsync` and `ProductCloner`. A Dapper write would bypass `IngredientCostPropagator`/`SubAssemblyCostPropagator`, leaving stored costs stale, and would surface a unique-name violation as `SqlException` rather than the `DbUpdateException` `ViewModelBase` maps to "That name is already in use." The pieces, in `Infrastructure/Queries/`: `DapperReads` borrows a DbContext's connection (so the connection string and the tests' `CreateDbContextFactory()` work unchanged; Dapper opens and closes it when EF hasn't) and has the factory- and context-level `QueryAsync`/`QuerySingleOrDefaultAsync`/`ReadAsync`; `Columns.Of<T>(alias)` is every entity's column list, **Id first** (a multi-mapped join splits on `Id`); `RawMaterialReads` loads materials with their category, base unit and units (largest first), alone or under product packaging lines or set lines; `SubAssemblyReads` loads sub-assemblies with their role and owner. **`Columns` must list exactly EF's columns**: a property left out reads back as its default with no error, which EF never did. `tests/Queries/ColumnsTests` compares every list against EF's design-time model (no database), so a new or renamed property fails that test until `Columns` has it; that's what `InternalsVisibleTo TinapayanRMS.Tests` on Infrastructure is for. Table names are the `DbSet` names and written into the SQL, so a table rename needs the SQL updated by hand (the persistence tests catch it). Mechanics: Dapper 2.1 maps `date` to `DateOnly` and a string column to an enum stored by name (`HasConversion<string>()`) on its own. Join an optional navigation with `LEFT JOIN`; Dapper returns null for a split whose first column (its `Id`) is null. A list of ids is one `@ids` parameter (`DapperReads.IdList`) read by `IN (DapperReads.IdsTable)` (`STRING_SPLIT`), so it never hits SQL Server's 2,100-parameter limit. A collection navigation (a material's `Units`, a pack's `Lines`) is a second query stitched on by id, not a join, because `GridReader`'s multi-mapping is synchronous only; everything else is one query. As with an untracked EF query, there's no identity resolution: a material on two lines is two objects, each with its own `Units` list. A read EF left unordered now has an explicit `ORDER BY` on its `Id` (packaging lines, usages, BOM lines, `GetByIdsAsync`), which is the order EF's collection-include queries produced anyway.
- **A ViewModel load that makes several reads runs them in one `Task.Run`** (2026-09-19, at the user's request). Each `await` on the UI thread comes back to it before the next read starts, and while WPF is drawing, each return waits its turn, so eight sequential reads cost eight waits. `ProductsViewModel.LoadProductDetailAsync` reads everything a product's detail shows in `ReadProductDetailAsync` (repositories only, no ViewModel state) inside one `Task.Run`, returns a `ProductDetail` record, and applies it after a single return to the UI thread; `RawMaterialsViewModel.LoadDetailsAsync` does the same for a raw material's price history, brand prices and BOM (`ReadDetailAsync`, `RawMaterialDetail`). Measured in the app against the dev database: the median click went from about 330 ms to 260 ms until the UI was idle, the first click from about 650 ms to 450 ms. The reads still show 45–100 ms although the SQL takes about 15 ms: that one return waits for WPF to finish drawing the cleared panel, which no change to the reads can remove. EF and Dapper were measured the same way and performed alike (roughly 150–800 ms a click, either way).
- **Correctness and performance take priority over brevity or convenience.** Favor the approach that is provably correct and doesn't waste allocations, database round-trips, or UI-thread work, even if a shorter or simpler alternative exists — e.g. querying only the columns/rows actually needed rather than materializing more and filtering in memory, and avoiding unnecessary intermediate collections in hot paths like the reactive costing cascade.
- This document is a living plan. Update it as phases complete or decisions change, so it stays the single source of truth for the project.
